FF was supported by JST BOOST Program Grant Number JPMJBY24G8. This work was supported by Japan Science and Technology Agency (JST) as part of Adopting Sustainable Partnerships for Innovative Research Ecosystem (ASPIRE), Grant Number JPMJAP25B1.

Appendix A
Proofs for the generic rewrite statements

A.1
Review of the risk rewrite

Starting from Eq. (
7
), write

R_{\ell}(f)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}L_{\ell}(x;f)^{\top}\,dB(x).

Substituting the decontamination identity
B=M_{\mathrm{corr}}^{\dagger}\bar{P}
from Eq. (
8
) gives

R_{\ell}(f)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}L_{\ell}(x;f)^{\top}M_{\mathrm{corr}}^{\dagger}\,d\bar{P}(x),

which is exactly Eq. (
9
).

The same substitution also covers the
x
-dependent case that appears in confidence-based weak supervision. Namely, whenever the clean conditional vector can be recovered through an
x
-dependent decontamination operator

B(dx)=M_{\mathrm{corr}}^{\dagger}(x)\,\bar{P}(dx),

(23)

we obtain the pointwise rewrite

R_{\ell}(f)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}L_{\ell}(x;f)^{\top}M_{\mathrm{corr}}^{\dagger}(x)\,d\bar{P}(x).

(24)

Thus the algebra of the contamination-matrix case remains unchanged except that the correction coefficients are now functions of
x
. In the terminology of
4
, this is the regime of confidence-based settings, where observable confidences provide the weights defining the contamination matrix. Section
3.2
treats Pconf as the representative binary example, and Appendix
B
records other instances such as posterior-confidence and Sconf supervision.

A.2
Proof of Theorem
1

By definition,

R_{c,w}(f)=\mathbb{E}[c(X)w(f(X))(Y-f(X))].

Fix
(c,w)
. By conditioning on
Y
, we obtain

\displaystyle R_{c,w}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{f}^{c,w}(X,Y)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{f}^{c,w}(X,Y)\mathbf{1}\{Y=1\}]+\mathbb{E}[\phi_{f}^{c,w}(X,Y)\mathbf{1}\{Y=0\}]

\displaystyle=P(Y=1)\mathbb{E}[\phi_{f}^{c,w}(X,1)\mid Y=1]+P(Y=0)\mathbb{E}[\phi_{f}^{c,w}(X,0)\mid Y=0]

\displaystyle=\pi_{+}\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[\phi_{f}^{c,w}(X,1)]+\pi_{-}\mathbb{E}_{P_{-}}[\phi_{f}^{c,w}(X,0)].

For a fixed witness
\phi_{f}
, define the corresponding vector of label-resolved terms

L_{c,w}(x;f):=\begin{pmatrix}\pi_{+}\phi_{f}(x,1)\\
\pi_{-}\phi_{f}(x,0)\end{pmatrix}.

Consequently, the witness moment is

R_{c,w}(f)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}L_{c,w}(x;f)^{\top}\,dB(x).

Substituting the decontamination identity
B=M_{\mathrm{corr}}^{\dagger}\bar{P}
from Eq. (
8
) gives

R_{c,w}(f)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}L_{c,w}(x;f)^{\top}M_{\mathrm{corr}}^{\dagger}\,d\bar{P}(x).

Taking the supremum over
(c,w)
yields Eq. (
15
).

Appendix B
Additional weak-supervision settings

This appendix collects the binary weak-supervision estimators induced by the contamination-based rewrite. Following the request of the main text, we omit multiclass cases and organize the appendix by weak-supervision model. In every case, the derivation follows the same template: first rewrite a fixed witness

R_{\phi}(f):=\pi_{+}\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[\phi_{f}(X,1)]+\pi_{-}\mathbb{E}_{P_{-}}[\phi_{f}(X,0)],

under the observable weak distributions, and then substitute the multicalibration witness

\phi_{f}^{c,w}(x,y)=c(x)w(f(x))(y-f(x)).

For later use, note that

\phi_{f}^{c,w}(x,1)=c(x)w(f(x))(1-f(x)),\qquad\phi_{f}^{c,w}(x,0)=-c(x)w(f(x))f(x).

B.1
MCD scenarios: UU template and special cases

We begin with the matrix-contamination settings. This subsection gives the derivation underlying the concise UU discussion in Section
3.2
.

Data model.

In the general UU setting we observe two unlabeled samples
X_{1}^{U_{1}},\dots,X_{n_{1}}^{U_{1}}\sim P_{U_{1}}
and
X_{1}^{U_{2}},\dots,X_{n_{2}}^{U_{2}}\sim P_{U_{2}}
. Write
\theta_{k}=P(Y=1\mid U_{k})
, so that
P_{U_{k}}=\theta_{k}P_{+}+(1-\theta_{k})P_{-}
. We use
B=(P_{+},P_{-})^{\top}
and
L_{\phi}(x)=(\pi_{+}\phi_{f}(x,1),\pi_{-}\phi_{f}(x,0))^{\top}
. Then

\bar{P}=\begin{pmatrix}P_{U_{1}}\\
P_{U_{2}}\end{pmatrix}=M_{\mathrm{UU}}B,\qquad M_{\mathrm{UU}}=\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{1}&1-\theta_{1}\\
\theta_{2}&1-\theta_{2}\end{pmatrix},

(25)

with
\Delta:=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\neq 0
. Hence

M_{\mathrm{UU}}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\Delta}\begin{pmatrix}1-\theta_{2}&-(1-\theta_{1})\\
-\theta_{2}&\theta_{1}\end{pmatrix}.

(26)

Multiplying
L_{\phi}(x)^{\top}
by
M_{\mathrm{UU}}^{-1}
gives the corrected witness row vector

\displaystyle L_{\phi}(x)^{\top}M_{\mathrm{UU}}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Delta}\Bigl((1-\theta_{2})\pi_{+}\phi_{f}(x,1)-\theta_{2}\pi_{-}\phi_{f}(x,0),

\displaystyle\hskip 79.66771pt-(1-\theta_{1})\pi_{+}\phi_{f}(x,1)+\theta_{1}\pi_{-}\phi_{f}(x,0)\Bigr).

(27)

Substituting Eq. (
27
) into the generic rewrite yields

\displaystyle R_{\phi}^{\mathrm{UU}}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{U_{1}}}\!\left[\frac{(1-\theta_{2})\pi_{+}}{\Delta}\phi_{f}(X,1)-\frac{\theta_{2}\pi_{-}}{\Delta}\phi_{f}(X,0)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{P_{U_{2}}}\!\left[-\frac{(1-\theta_{1})\pi_{+}}{\Delta}\phi_{f}(X,1)+\frac{\theta_{1}\pi_{-}}{\Delta}\phi_{f}(X,0)\right].

(28)

For the multicalibration witness, define

\alpha_{1}(v):=\frac{(1-\theta_{2})\pi_{+}(1-v)+\theta_{2}\pi_{-}v}{\Delta},\qquad\alpha_{2}(v):=\frac{-(1-\theta_{1})\pi_{+}(1-v)-\theta_{1}\pi_{-}v}{\Delta}.

With this notation,

\displaystyle R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{UU}}(f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{P_{U_{k}}}\!\left[\alpha_{k}(f(X))c(X)w(f(X))\right],

(29)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{UU}}(f)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\alpha_{k}(f(X_{i}^{U_{k}}))c(X_{i}^{U_{k}})w(f(X_{i}^{U_{k}})).

(30)

The remaining MCD-type estimators are obtained by substituting the appropriate positive-class proportions into Eq. (
28
)–(
30
). Unless stated otherwise, these MCD-style rewrites assume that the class prior
\pi_{+}
and the weak-source mixing parameters appearing in the model are known or have been pre-estimated. We write the resulting fixed-witness identities explicitly below. In the similar/dissimilar settings,
P_{\tilde{S}}
and
P_{\tilde{D}}
denote the observable one-point marginals induced by similar and dissimilar pair supervision, namely

P_{\tilde{S}}=\frac{\pi_{+}^{2}}{\pi_{+}^{2}+\pi_{-}^{2}}P_{+}+\frac{\pi_{-}^{2}}{\pi_{+}^{2}+\pi_{-}^{2}}P_{-},\qquad P_{\tilde{D}}=\frac{1}{2}P_{+}+\frac{1}{2}P_{-}.

PU learning.

Observed data.
We observe a positively labeled sample
X_{1}^{+},\dots,X_{n_{+}}^{+}\sim P_{+}
and an unlabeled sample
X_{1}^{u},\dots,X_{n_{u}}^{u}\sim P_{X}=\pi_{+}P_{+}+\pi_{-}P_{-}
. We assume the class prior
\pi_{+}
is known or pre-estimated. This is the UU specialization
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=(1,\pi_{+})
.
Set
\theta_{1}=1
and
\theta_{2}=\pi_{+}
, so that

\bar{P}=\begin{pmatrix}P_{+}\\
P_{X}\end{pmatrix},\qquad M_{\mathrm{PU}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\pi_{+}&\pi_{-}\end{pmatrix},\qquad M_{\mathrm{PU}}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\pi_{+}/\pi_{-}&1/\pi_{-}\end{pmatrix}.

Hence the PU fixed-witness rewrite is

\displaystyle R_{\phi}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)

\displaystyle=\pi_{+}\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[\phi_{f}(X,1)-\phi_{f}(X,0)]+\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}[\phi_{f}(X,0)].

(31)

Since
\phi_{f}^{c,w}(x,1)-\phi_{f}^{c,w}(x,0)=c(x)w(f(x))
, it follows that

\displaystyle R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)

\displaystyle=\pi_{+}\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[c(X)w(f(X))]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}[c(X)w(f(X))f(X)],

(32)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)

\displaystyle:=\pi_{+}\frac{1}{n_{+}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{+}}c(X_{i}^{+})w(f(X_{i}^{+}))-\frac{1}{n_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{u}}c(X_{j}^{u})w(f(X_{j}^{u}))f(X_{j}^{u}).

(33)

SU learning.

Observed data.
We observe i.i.d. similar pairs
(X_{i}^{s},X_{i}^{s\prime})
known to share the same label, together with unlabeled points
X_{1}^{u},\dots,X_{n_{u}}^{u}\sim P_{X}
. Let
X_{1}^{\tilde{S}},\dots,X_{n_{s}}^{\tilde{S}}\sim P_{\tilde{S}}
denote the induced one-point marginal of the similar-pair sample. The rewrite below is written in terms of this observable marginal.
Set
\theta_{1}=\pi_{+}^{2}/(\pi_{+}^{2}+\pi_{-}^{2})
and
\theta_{2}=\pi_{+}
. Then

\displaystyle R_{\phi}^{\mathrm{SU}}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\tilde{S}}}\!\left[\frac{\pi_{+}^{2}+\pi_{-}^{2}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\bigl(\phi_{f}(X,1)-\phi_{f}(X,0)\bigr)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}\!\left[-\frac{\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\phi_{f}(X,1)+\frac{\pi_{+}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\phi_{f}(X,0)\right].

(34)

Substituting
\phi_{f}^{c,w}
gives

\displaystyle R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{SU}}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{+}^{2}+\pi_{-}^{2}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\tilde{S}}}[c(X)w(f(X))]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}\!\left[c(X)w(f(X))\left(\frac{\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}+f(X)\right)\right],

(35)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{SU}}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\pi_{+}^{2}+\pi_{-}^{2}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\frac{1}{n_{s}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{s}}c(X_{i}^{\tilde{S}})w(f(X_{i}^{\tilde{S}}))

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{n_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{u}}c(X_{j}^{u})w(f(X_{j}^{u}))\left(\frac{\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}+f(X_{j}^{u})\right).

(36)

DU learning.

Observed data.
We observe i.i.d. dissimilar pairs
(X_{i}^{d},X_{i}^{d\prime})
known to have different labels, together with unlabeled points
X_{1}^{u},\dots,X_{n_{u}}^{u}\sim P_{X}
. Let
X_{1}^{\tilde{D}},\dots,X_{n_{d}}^{\tilde{D}}\sim P_{\tilde{D}}
denote the induced one-point marginal of the dissimilar-pair sample. The rewrite below is written in terms of this observable marginal.
Set
\theta_{1}=1/2
and
\theta_{2}=\pi_{+}
. Then

\displaystyle R_{\phi}^{\mathrm{DU}}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\tilde{D}}}\!\left[\frac{2\pi_{+}\pi_{-}}{\pi_{-}-\pi_{+}}\bigl(\phi_{f}(X,1)-\phi_{f}(X,0)\bigr)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}\!\left[-\frac{\pi_{+}}{\pi_{-}-\pi_{+}}\phi_{f}(X,1)+\frac{\pi_{-}}{\pi_{-}-\pi_{+}}\phi_{f}(X,0)\right].

(37)

Therefore the DU witness moment is

\displaystyle R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{DU}}(f)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\pi_{+}\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\tilde{D}}}[c(X)w(f(X))]+\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}\!\left[c(X)w(f(X))\left(\frac{\pi_{+}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}-f(X)\right)\right],

(38)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{DU}}(f)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{2\pi_{+}\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\frac{1}{n_{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{d}}c(X_{i}^{\tilde{D}})w(f(X_{i}^{\tilde{D}}))

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{n_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{u}}c(X_{j}^{u})w(f(X_{j}^{u}))\left(\frac{\pi_{+}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}-f(X_{j}^{u})\right).

(39)

SD learning.

Observed data.
We observe both i.i.d. similar pairs
(X_{i}^{s},X_{i}^{s\prime})
and i.i.d. dissimilar pairs
(X_{j}^{d},X_{j}^{d\prime})
. Let
X_{1}^{\tilde{S}},\dots,X_{n_{s}}^{\tilde{S}}\sim P_{\tilde{S}}
and
X_{1}^{\tilde{D}},\dots,X_{n_{d}}^{\tilde{D}}\sim P_{\tilde{D}}
denote the corresponding one-point marginals used below.
Set
\theta_{1}=\pi_{+}^{2}/(\pi_{+}^{2}+\pi_{-}^{2})
and
\theta_{2}=1/2
. Then

\displaystyle R_{\phi}^{\mathrm{SD}}(f)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{+}^{2}+\pi_{-}^{2})\mathbb{E}_{P_{\tilde{S}}}\!\left[\frac{\pi_{+}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\phi_{f}(X,1)-\frac{\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\phi_{f}(X,0)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+2\pi_{+}\pi_{-}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\tilde{D}}}\!\left[-\frac{\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\phi_{f}(X,1)+\frac{\pi_{+}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\phi_{f}(X,0)\right].

(40)

Therefore the SD witness moment is

\displaystyle R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{SD}}(f)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{+}^{2}+\pi_{-}^{2})\mathbb{E}_{P_{\tilde{S}}}\!\left[c(X)w(f(X))\left(\frac{\pi_{+}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}-f(X)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad-2\pi_{+}\pi_{-}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\tilde{D}}}\!\left[c(X)w(f(X))\left(\frac{\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}+f(X)\right)\right],

(41)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{SD}}(f)

\displaystyle:=(\pi_{+}^{2}+\pi_{-}^{2})\frac{1}{n_{s}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{s}}c(X_{i}^{\tilde{S}})w(f(X_{i}^{\tilde{S}}))\left(\frac{\pi_{+}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}-f(X_{i}^{\tilde{S}})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-2\pi_{+}\pi_{-}\frac{1}{n_{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{d}}c(X_{j}^{\tilde{D}})w(f(X_{j}^{\tilde{D}}))\left(\frac{\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}+f(X_{j}^{\tilde{D}})\right).

(42)

B.2
Pairwise comparison learning

Observed data.
We observe comparison pairs carrying a superior/inferior outcome rather than a clean binary label. Let
X_{1}^{\mathrm{sup}},\dots,X_{n_{\mathrm{sup}}}^{\mathrm{sup}}\sim P_{\mathrm{Sup}}
and
X_{1}^{\mathrm{inf}},\dots,X_{n_{\mathrm{inf}}}^{\mathrm{inf}}\sim P_{\mathrm{Inf}}
denote the induced one-point marginals associated with superior and inferior events, respectively. The fixed-witness rewrite is written in terms of these observable marginals. The unified-risk rewrite is expressed through the corrected losses

\bar{\ell}_{\mathrm{Sup}}=\ell_{1}-\pi_{+}\ell_{0},\qquad\bar{\ell}_{\mathrm{Inf}}=-\pi_{-}\ell_{1}+\ell_{0},

where
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Sup}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Inf}}
denote expectations over
P_{\mathrm{Sup}}
and
P_{\mathrm{Inf}}
. Replacing
(\ell_{1},\ell_{0})
by
(\phi_{f}(\cdot,1),\phi_{f}(\cdot,0))
yields

\displaystyle R_{\phi}^{\mathrm{Pcomp}}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Sup}}[\phi_{f}(X,1)-\pi_{+}\phi_{f}(X,0)]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Inf}}[-\pi_{-}\phi_{f}(X,1)+\phi_{f}(X,0)].

(43)

Thus the comparison-learning witness moment is

\displaystyle R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{Pcomp}}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Sup}}[c(X)w(f(X))(1-\pi_{-}f(X))]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Inf}}[c(X)w(f(X))(\pi_{-}+\pi_{+}f(X))],

(44)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{Pcomp}}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{sup}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{sup}}}c(X_{i}^{\mathrm{sup}})w(f(X_{i}^{\mathrm{sup}}))(1-\pi_{-}f(X_{i}^{\mathrm{sup}}))

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{inf}}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\mathrm{inf}}}c(X_{j}^{\mathrm{inf}})w(f(X_{j}^{\mathrm{inf}}))(\pi_{-}+\pi_{+}f(X_{j}^{\mathrm{inf}})).

(45)

B.3
Confidence-based binary settings

We next collect binary settings whose decontamination rule depends on observed confidences.

Posterior-confidence learning.

Observed data.
We observe i.i.d. pairs
(X_{i},r_{i})
with
X_{i}\sim P_{X}
and
r_{i}=r(X_{i})=P(Y=1\mid X_{i})
. In contrast to Pconf, the sample is drawn from the population marginal rather than from
P_{+}
. Conditioning on
X
directly gives

\displaystyle R_{\phi}^{\mathrm{PC}}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X}\big[r(X)\phi_{f}(X,1)+(1-r(X))\phi_{f}(X,0)\big].

(46)

Specializing to
\phi_{f}^{c,w}
yields

\displaystyle R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PC}}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X}\big[c(X)w(f(X))(r(X)-f(X))\big],

(47)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PC}}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c(X_{i})w(f(X_{i}))(r_{i}-f(X_{i})).

(48)

Thus this supervision model replaces the inaccessible Bernoulli outcome
Y
by the observed posterior mean
r(X)
.

Pconf learning.

Observed data.
We observe i.i.d. positive-confidence pairs
(X_{i},r_{i})
with
X_{i}\sim P_{+}
and
r_{i}=r(X_{i})=P(Y=1\mid X_{i})
. We also assume the class prior
\pi_{+}
is known or pre-estimated, because the exact witness moment keeps the original population scaling. The exact rewrite requires
P_{-}\ll P_{+}
. In the
B=(P_{+},P_{-})^{\top}
notation of Section
2.2
, this corresponds to the
x
-dependent decontamination operator
M_{\mathrm{Pconf}}^{\dagger}(x)=\mathrm{diag}\{1,(\pi_{+}/\pi_{-})(1-r(x))/r(x)\}
acting on the duplicated positive source
\bar{P}_{\mathrm{Pconf}}=(P_{+},P_{+})^{\top}
. Bayes’ rule implies the density-ratio identity

\pi_{-}P_{-}(dx)=\pi_{+}\frac{1-r(x)}{r(x)}P_{+}(dx).

(49)

Substituting Eq. (
49
) into the label-resolved expansion of
R_{\phi}(f)
gives

\displaystyle R_{\phi}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}(f)

\displaystyle=\pi_{+}\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}\!\left[\phi_{f}(X,1)+\frac{1-r(X)}{r(X)}\phi_{f}(X,0)\right].

(50)

For the multicalibration witness,

\displaystyle R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}(f)

\displaystyle=\pi_{+}\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}\!\left[c(X)w(f(X))\left(1-\frac{f(X)}{r(X)}\right)\right],

(51)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\pi_{+}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c(X_{i})w(f(X_{i}))\left(1-\frac{f(X_{i})}{r_{i}}\right).

(52)

This is the exact fixed-witness estimator used in the main text.

Sconf learning.

Observed data.
We observe i.i.d. triples

(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime},r_{1}),\dots,(X_{n_{\mathrm{sc}}},X_{n_{\mathrm{sc}}}^{\prime},r_{n_{\mathrm{sc}}}),\qquad(X_{i},X_{i}^{\prime})\sim P_{XX^{\prime}},\qquad r_{i}=r(X_{i},X_{i}^{\prime}):=P(Y=1\mid X_{i},X_{i}^{\prime}).

As in the formulas below, the class prior
\pi_{+}
is assumed known or pre-estimated. In contrast to Pconf, the side information is attached to a pair rather than to a single positive example. The unified-risk rewrite gives

\displaystyle R_{\phi}^{\mathrm{Sconf}}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(X,X^{\prime},r)}\Biggl[\frac{r-\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\frac{\phi_{f}(X,1)+\phi_{f}(X^{\prime},1)}{2}+\frac{\pi_{+}-r}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\frac{\phi_{f}(X,0)+\phi_{f}(X^{\prime},0)}{2}\Biggr].

(53)

Substituting
\phi_{f}^{c,w}
yields

\displaystyle R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{Sconf}}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(X,X^{\prime},r)}\Biggl[\frac{r-\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\frac{c(X)w(f(X))(1-f(X))+c(X^{\prime})w(f(X^{\prime}))(1-f(X^{\prime}))}{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt-\frac{\pi_{+}-r}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\frac{c(X)w(f(X))f(X)+c(X^{\prime})w(f(X^{\prime}))f(X^{\prime})}{2}\Biggr],

(54)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{Sconf}}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{sc}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{sc}}}\Biggl[\frac{r_{i}-\pi_{-}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\frac{c(X_{i})w(f(X_{i}))(1-f(X_{i}))+c(X_{i}^{\prime})w(f(X_{i}^{\prime}))(1-f(X_{i}^{\prime}))}{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt-\frac{\pi_{+}-r_{i}}{\pi_{+}-\pi_{-}}\frac{c(X_{i})w(f(X_{i}))f(X_{i})+c(X_{i}^{\prime})w(f(X_{i}^{\prime}))f(X_{i}^{\prime})}{2}\Biggr].

(55)

Summary.

The message of this appendix is that every binary weak-supervision model gives a fixed-witness estimator by exactly the same substitution principle: rewrite
R_{\phi}(f)
using the decontamination rule of the model, then insert the witness
\phi_{f}^{c,w}
. The resulting empirical multicalibration criterion is always

\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}^{\,\mathrm{WSL}}(f):=\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\mathcal{W}}\bigl|\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{WSL}}(f)\bigr|,

with
\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{WSL}}(f)
chosen from Eq. (
30
), Eq. (
33
), (
36
), (
39
), (
42
), (
45
), (
48
), (
52
), or (
55
) according to the observation model.

Appendix C
Proofs of the uniform convergence

Throughout this appendix,
f
is fixed independently of the weak sample; We write

\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{C};P):=\mathbb{E}_{S\sim P^{n},\,\sigma}\left[\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}c(X_{i})\right]

for the expected Rademacher complexity of
\mathcal{C}
under
P
, where
\sigma_{i}\in\{-1,+1\}
are independent Rademacher variables.

C.1
Uniform convergence for UU

For the UU estimator in Eq. (
20
), define

\operatorname{MC}^{\mathrm{UU}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f):=\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\mathcal{W}}|R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{UU}}(f)|,\qquad\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{\mathrm{UU}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f):=\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\mathcal{W}}|\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{UU}}(f)|.

Define the source envelopes

A_{1}:=\sup_{v\in[0,1]}|\alpha_{1}(v)|,\qquad A_{2}:=\sup_{v\in[0,1]}|\alpha_{2}(v)|.

Proposition 2

(Uniform convergence for the UU weak estimate)
.

Assume
|c(x)|\leq 1
for all
c\in\mathcal{C}
and
|w(v)|\leq 1
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then for any
\eta\in(0,1]
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{\mathrm{UU}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)-\operatorname{MC}^{\mathrm{UU}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)\right|\leq 2\sum_{k=1}^{2}A_{k}\left(\mathfrak{R}_{n_{k}}(\mathcal{C};P_{U_{k}})+\eta+\sqrt{\frac{2\log N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})+\log(4/\delta)}{n_{k}}}\right).

Proof of Proposition
2
.

Fix
\eta\in(0,1]
, and let

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\subseteq\mathcal{W}
be a minimal
\eta
-net of

\mathcal{W}
under
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, so that

|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}|=N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W}).

For
k\in\{1,2\}
and
w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
, define the
source-specific classes

\mathcal{G}_{k,w}(f):=\left\{x\mapsto\alpha_{k}(f(x))w(f(x))c(x):c\in\mathcal{C}\right\},\qquad\mathcal{G}_{k}(f):=\bigcup_{w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}}\mathcal{G}_{k,w}(f).

By construction, every function in
\mathcal{G}_{k}(f)
is bounded in
absolute value by
A_{k}
.

By symmetrization and bounded-differences concentration, with
probability at least
1-\delta
, simultaneously for
k=1,2
,

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{k}(f)}\left|\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}g(X_{i}^{U_{k}})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{U_{k}}}[g(X)]\right|\leq 2\mathfrak{R}_{n_{k}}(\mathcal{G}_{k}(f);P_{U_{k}})+A_{k}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(4/\delta)}{n_{k}}}.

We next bound the Rademacher complexity of
\mathcal{G}_{k}(f)
by the
complexity of
\mathcal{C}
and the size of the weight net. For a fixed

w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
and a fixed sample

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{k}}
, the maps

\psi_{i}(t):=\alpha_{k}(f(x_{i}))w(f(x_{i}))t

satisfy
\psi_{i}(0)=0
and are
A_{k}
-Lipschitz. Therefore the
contraction inequality gives

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n_{k}}(\mathcal{G}_{k,w}(f))\leq A_{k}\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n_{k}}(\mathcal{C}).

We use the following finite-union consequence of the sub-Gaussian
maximal inequality. Conditional on a sample
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
, let

\mathcal{F}=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{M}\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
and suppose that

|h(x_{i})|\leq\lambda
for all
h\in\mathcal{F}
and all
i
.
Define

Z_{\ell}:=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}h(x_{i}),\qquad\ell=1,\ldots,M.

Then
Z_{\ell}-\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}Z_{\ell}
is

\lambda/\sqrt{m}
-sub-Gaussian by the bounded-difference inequality.
Therefore, the sub-Gaussian maximal inequality gives

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}(\mathcal{F})=\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\max_{\ell\leq M}Z_{\ell}\leq\max_{\ell\leq M}\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}(\mathcal{F}_{\ell})+\lambda\sqrt{\frac{2\log M}{m}}.

Applying this with

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{G}_{k}(f),\qquad\mathcal{F}_{\ell}=\mathcal{G}_{k,w_{\ell}}(f),\qquad M=|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}|=N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W}),\qquad\lambda=A_{k},

and then taking expectations over the
U_{k}
-sample, yields

\mathfrak{R}_{n_{k}}(\mathcal{G}_{k}(f);P_{U_{k}})\leq A_{k}\mathfrak{R}_{n_{k}}(\mathcal{C};P_{U_{k}})+A_{k}\sqrt{\frac{2\log N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})}{n_{k}}}.

Combining the preceding displays gives

\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}}\left|\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{UU}}(f)-R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{UU}}(f)\right|\leq 2\sum_{k=1}^{2}A_{k}\left(\mathfrak{R}_{n_{k}}(\mathcal{C};P_{U_{k}})+\sqrt{\frac{2\log N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})+\log(4/\delta)}{n_{k}}}\right),

after absorbing universal numerical constants.

It remains to pass from the net
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
to the full class

\mathcal{W}
. For any
w\in\mathcal{W}
, choose

\widetilde{w}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
such that

\|w-\widetilde{w}\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
. Then, for each

k\in\{1,2\}
, uniformly over
c\in\mathcal{C}
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{P_{U_{k}}}\left[\alpha_{k}(f(X))c(X)\{w(f(X))-\widetilde{w}(f(X))\}\right]\right|\leq A_{k}\eta,

and the same bound holds for the empirical average,

\left|\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\alpha_{k}(f(X_{i}^{U_{k}}))c(X_{i}^{U_{k}})\{w(f(X_{i}^{U_{k}}))-\widetilde{w}(f(X_{i}^{U_{k}}))\}\right|\leq A_{k}\eta.

Thus the approximation error from replacing
w
by

\widetilde{w}
is at most
2A_{k}\eta
for source
k
. Therefore,

\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\mathcal{W}}\left|\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{UU}}(f)-R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{UU}}(f)\right|\leq 2\sum_{k=1}^{2}A_{k}\left(\mathfrak{R}_{n_{k}}(\mathcal{C};P_{U_{k}})+\eta+\sqrt{\frac{2\log N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})+\log(4/\delta)}{n_{k}}}\right).

Finally,

\left|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{\mathrm{UU}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)-\operatorname{MC}^{\mathrm{UU}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)\right|\leq\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\mathcal{W}}\left|\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{UU}}(f)-R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{UU}}(f)\right|,

which completes the proof.
∎

C.2
Uniform convergence for PU

This section proves Proposition
1
. The proof is written directly for the two PU sources, rather than by introducing the composed class
x\mapsto c(x)w(f(x))
.

Proof of Proposition
1
.

Fix
\eta\in(0,1]
, and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\subseteq\mathcal{W}
be a minimal
\eta
-net of
\mathcal{W}
under
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, so that

|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}|=N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W}).

For
w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
, define the source-specific classes

\mathcal{G}_{+,w}(f):=\left\{x\mapsto\pi_{+}\,w(f(x))c(x):c\in\mathcal{C}\right\},\qquad\mathcal{G}_{u,w}(f):=\left\{x\mapsto f(x)w(f(x))c(x):c\in\mathcal{C}\right\},

and set

\mathcal{G}_{+}(f):=\bigcup_{w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}}\mathcal{G}_{+,w}(f),\qquad\mathcal{G}_{u}(f):=\bigcup_{w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}}\mathcal{G}_{u,w}(f).

Every function in
\mathcal{G}_{+}(f)
is bounded by
\pi_{+}
in absolute value, and every function in
\mathcal{G}_{u}(f)
is bounded by
1
in absolute value.

By symmetrization and bounded-differences concentration, with probability at least
1-\delta
, simultaneously for the positive and unlabeled samples,

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{+}(f)}\left|\frac{1}{n_{+}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{+}}g(X_{i}^{+})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[g(X)]\right|\leq 2\mathfrak{R}_{n_{+}}(\mathcal{G}_{+}(f);P_{+})+\pi_{+}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(4/\delta)}{n_{+}}},

and, for the unlabeled source,

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{u}(f)}\left|\frac{1}{n_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{u}}g(X_{j}^{u})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}[g(X)]\right|\leq 2\mathfrak{R}_{n_{u}}(\mathcal{G}_{u}(f);P_{X})+\sqrt{\frac{2\log(4/\delta)}{n_{u}}}.

We next bound the two Rademacher complexities by the complexity of

\mathcal{C}
and the size of the weight net. For a fixed

w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
and a fixed positive sample

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{+}}
, the maps

\psi_{i}(t):=\pi_{+}\,w(f(x_{i}))\,t

satisfy
\psi_{i}(0)=0
and are
\pi_{+}
-Lipschitz. Therefore the
contraction inequality gives

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n_{+}}(\mathcal{G}_{+,w}(f))\leq\pi_{+}\,\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n_{+}}(\mathcal{C}).

We use the following finite-union consequence of the sub-Gaussian
maximal inequality. Conditional on a sample
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
, let

\mathcal{F}=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{M}\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
and suppose that

|h(x_{i})|\leq\lambda
for all
h\in\mathcal{F}
and all
i
.
Define

Z_{\ell}:=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}h(x_{i}),\qquad\ell=1,\ldots,M.

Then
Z_{\ell}-\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}Z_{\ell}
is

\lambda/\sqrt{m}
-sub-Gaussian by the bounded-difference inequality.
Therefore, the sub-Gaussian maximal inequality gives

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}(\mathcal{F})=\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\max_{\ell\leq M}Z_{\ell}\leq\max_{\ell\leq M}\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}(\mathcal{F}_{\ell})+\lambda\sqrt{\frac{2\log M}{m}}.

Applying this with
M=|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}|=N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})
and

\lambda=\pi_{+}
, and then taking expectations over the positive
sample, yields

\mathfrak{R}_{n_{+}}(\mathcal{G}_{+}(f);P_{+})\leq\pi_{+}\,\mathfrak{R}_{n_{+}}(\mathcal{C};P_{+})+\pi_{+}\sqrt{\frac{2\log N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})}{n_{+}}}.

Similarly, for the unlabeled sample, the maps

\psi_{i}(t):=f(x_{i})w(f(x_{i}))\,t

satisfy
\psi_{i}(0)=0
and are
1
-Lipschitz, because

0\leq f\leq 1
and
\|w\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Hence, by the contraction
inequality and the same finite-union argument with

M=|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}|=N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})
and
\lambda=1
,

\mathfrak{R}_{n_{u}}(\mathcal{G}_{u}(f);P_{X})\leq\mathfrak{R}_{n_{u}}(\mathcal{C};P_{X})+\sqrt{\frac{2\log N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})}{n_{u}}}.

Using the shorthand

(Q_{+},m_{+},\lambda_{+}):=(P_{+},n_{+},\pi_{+}),\qquad(Q_{u},m_{u},\lambda_{u}):=(P_{X},n_{u},1),

the preceding displays give

\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}}\left|\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)-R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)\right|\leq 2\sum_{s\in\{+,u\}}\lambda_{s}\left(\mathfrak{R}_{m_{s}}(\mathcal{C};Q_{s})+\sqrt{\frac{2\log N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})+\log(4/\delta)}{m_{s}}}\right).

It remains to pass from
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
to
\mathcal{W}
. For any
w\in\mathcal{W}
, choose
\widetilde{w}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\|w-\widetilde{w}\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
. Then, uniformly over
c\in\mathcal{C}
,

\left|\pi_{+}\,\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}\left[c(X)\{w(f(X))-\widetilde{w}(f(X))\}\right]\right|\leq\pi_{+}\eta,

and the same bound holds for the empirical positive average. Likewise,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}\left[c(X)\{w(f(X))-\widetilde{w}(f(X))\}f(X)\right]\right|\leq\eta,

and the same bound holds for the empirical unlabeled average. Absorbing these approximation errors yields the bound in Eq. (
18
). Finally,

\left|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{\mathrm{PU}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)-\operatorname{MC}^{\mathrm{PU}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)\right|\leq\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\mathcal{W}}\left|\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)-R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)\right|,

which completes the proof.
∎

Example rates.

The same bound yields explicit rates for other common witness choices. If
n=n_{+}=n_{u}
,
\mathcal{W}
is finite, and
\mathcal{C}
has VC dimension
d
, the VC/Rademacher bound gives

\bigl|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{\mathrm{PU}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)-\operatorname{MC}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)\bigr|=O_{p}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log|\mathcal{W}|}{n}}\right).

If
\mathcal{C}=\{1\}
and
\mathcal{W}=\mathrm{Lip}_{1}([0,1],[-1,1])
, corresponding to SmoothCE, the preceding single-scale covering bound is not sharp. Indeed, since
\log N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})=O(1/\eta)
, optimizing Eq. (
18
) over one scale gives only
O_{p}(n^{-1/3})
. For SmoothCE we instead use Dudley’s chaining bound for the one-dimensional Lipschitz witness class. Define

\mathcal{G}_{1}:=\{z\mapsto w(z):w\in\mathcal{W}\},\qquad\mathcal{G}_{2}:=\{z\mapsto zw(z):w\in\mathcal{W}\}.

Then
\mathcal{G}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Lip}_{1}([0,1],[-1,1])
. Moreover, for
w\in\mathcal{W}
, the map
z\mapsto zw(z)
is bounded by one and is
2
-Lipschitz, since

|zw(z)-z^{\prime}w(z^{\prime})|\leq|z-z^{\prime}|\,|w(z)|+z^{\prime}|w(z)-w(z^{\prime})|\leq 2|z-z^{\prime}|.

Hence, for
k=1,2
, there is a universal constant
C
such that

\log N_{\infty}(u,\mathcal{G}_{k})\leq\frac{C}{u},\qquad u\in(0,1].

For a positive sample
X_{1}^{+},\ldots,X_{m}^{+}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P_{+}
and an unlabeled sample
X_{1}^{u},\ldots,X_{m}^{u}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P_{X}
, write

\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{+},m}[\varphi(X)]:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\varphi(X_{i}^{+}),\qquad\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{X},m}[\varphi(X)]:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\varphi(X_{i}^{u}).

Dudley’s entropy integral, with the empirical
L_{2}
entropy bounded by the sup-norm entropy, gives, for each
k=1,2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(X_{i}^{+})\sim P_{+}^{m}}\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{k}}\left|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{+},m}[g(f(X))]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[g(f(X))]\right|

(56)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathfrak{R}_{m}(\mathcal{G}_{k}\circ f;P_{+})\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{m}}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{\infty}(u,\mathcal{G}_{k})}\,du\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{m}},

(57)

and similarly

\mathbb{E}_{(X_{i}^{u})\sim P_{X}^{m}}\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{k}}\left|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{X},m}[g(f(X))]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}[g(f(X))]\right|\leq 2\mathfrak{R}_{m}(\mathcal{G}_{k}\circ f;P_{X})\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{m}}.

Here we used
\int_{0}^{1}u^{-1/2}du<\infty
. Since all functions in
\mathcal{G}_{k}
are uniformly bounded by one, bounded-difference concentration yields, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{k}}\left|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{+},m}[g(f(X))]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[g(f(X))]\right|\leq C\sqrt{\frac{1+\log(1/\delta)}{m}},

and the same bound holds with
P_{+}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{+},m}
replaced by
P_{X}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{X},m}
.
For the PU residual with
\mathcal{C}=\{1\}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{1,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)-R_{1,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)

(58)

\displaystyle=\pi_{+}\Bigl\{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{+},n_{+}}[w(f(X))]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[w(f(X))]\Bigr\}-\Bigl\{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{X},n_{u}}[f(X)w(f(X))]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}[f(X)w(f(X))]\Bigr\}.

(59)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\left|\widehat{R}_{1,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)-R_{1,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)\right|

(60)

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{+}\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{1}}\left|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{+},n_{+}}[g(f(X))]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[g(f(X))]\right|+\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{2}}\left|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{X},n_{u}}[g(f(X))]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}[g(f(X))]\right|.

(61)

Together with

\left|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{\mathrm{PU}}_{\{1\},\mathcal{W}}(f)-\operatorname{MC}_{\{1\},\mathcal{W}}(f)\right|\leq\sup_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\left|\widehat{R}_{1,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)-R_{1,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)\right|,

a union bound over the positive and unlabeled empirical processes gives, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{\mathrm{PU}}_{\{1\},\mathcal{W}}(f)-\operatorname{MC}_{\{1\},\mathcal{W}}(f)\right|\leq C\left\{\pi_{+}\sqrt{\frac{1+\log(2/\delta)}{n_{+}}}+\sqrt{\frac{1+\log(2/\delta)}{n_{u}}}\right\}.

In particular, if
n_{+}=n_{u}=n
, then

\bigl|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{\mathrm{PU}}_{\{1\},\mathcal{W}}(f)-\operatorname{MC}_{\{1\},\mathcal{W}}(f)\bigr|=O_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

This result matches the order of smooth CE in
14
.

Remark 1

(VC-type dimension)
.

We use the phrase “VC dimension” in the usual shorthand sense.
When
\mathcal{C}
is a class of indicator functions, this is the ordinary
VC dimension. For a general
[0,1]
-valued subgroup class
\mathcal{C}
,
the corresponding assumption should be interpreted as finite
VC-subgraph dimension, equivalently finite pseudo-dimension up to
standard convention-dependent constants. Under this condition the
standard Rademacher bound gives

\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{C};P)=O\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}\right),

which is the complexity term used in the example rates.

C.3
Uniform convergence for Pconf

This section states and proves the Pconf uniform convergence bound. The setup matches
24
: the Pconf sample consists of i.i.d. draws
(X_{i},r_{i})
with
X_{i}\sim P_{+}
and
r_{i}=r(X_{i})=P(Y=1\mid X_{i})
.

Proposition 3

(Uniform convergence for the Pconf weak estimate)
.

Define the population and empirical Pconf weak-estimate errors by

\operatorname{MC}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f):=\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\mathcal{W}}|R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}(f)|,\qquad\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f):=\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\mathcal{W}}|\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}(f)|.

Assume
f
is fixed independently of the Pconf sample,
|c(x)|\leq 1
for all
c\in\mathcal{C}
,
|w(v)|\leq 1
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, and there exists
C_{r}>0
such that
r(x)\geq C_{r}
almost surely under
P_{+}
. Then for any
\eta\in(0,1]
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)-\operatorname{MC}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)\right|\leq C\,\pi_{+}\!\left(1+\frac{1}{C_{r}}\right)\left(\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{C};P_{+})+\eta+\sqrt{\frac{2\log N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}\right).

Proof.

Fix
\eta\in(0,1]
and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\subseteq\mathcal{W}
be a minimal
\eta
-net of
\mathcal{W}
under
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, so
|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}|=N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})
. Define

\mathcal{G}_{w}(f):=\left\{x\mapsto\pi_{+}\left(1-\frac{f(x)}{r(x)}\right)w(f(x))c(x):c\in\mathcal{C}\right\},\qquad\mathcal{G}(f):=\bigcup_{w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}}\mathcal{G}_{w}(f).

By Corollary
3
,

\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}}\left|\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}(f)-R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}(f)\right|=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}(f)}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[g(X)]\right|.

Because
0\leq f\leq 1
,
|w|\leq 1
, and
r(x)\geq C_{r}
almost surely, every
g\in\mathcal{G}(f)
satisfies

|g(x)|\leq B:=\pi_{+}\left(1+\frac{1}{C_{r}}\right)\qquad P_{+}\text{-a.s.}

Standard symmetrization and McDiarmid’s inequality therefore give, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}(f)}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[g(X)]\right|\leq 2\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{G}(f);P_{+})+B\sqrt{\frac{2\log(4/\delta)}{n}}.

For a fixed
w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
, the maps

\psi_{i}(t):=\pi_{+}\left(1-\frac{f(x_{i})}{r(x_{i})}\right)w(f(x_{i}))t

satisfy
\psi_{i}(0)=0
and are
B
-Lipschitz, so the contraction inequality gives
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n}(\mathcal{G}_{w}(f))\leq B\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n}(\mathcal{C})
. Taking expectations and applying the finite-union lemma over
|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}|=N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})
classes yields

\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{G}(f);P_{+})\leq B\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{C};P_{+})+B\sqrt{\frac{2\log N_{\infty}(\eta,\mathcal{W})}{n}}.

It remains to move from the net back to the full class. For any
w\in\mathcal{W}
, choose
\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
with
\|w-\tilde{w}\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
. Then

\sup_{c}\left|\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}\!\left[\pi_{+}\left(1-\frac{f(X)}{r(X)}\right)c(X)(w(f(X))-\tilde{w}(f(X)))\right]\right|\leq B\eta,

and the same estimate holds for the empirical average. Absorbing these approximation errors proves the stated bound.
∎

Clipped Pconf estimator.

The Pconf rewrite in Corollary
3
is exact but can have large variance when some confidences are small. A standard stabilization is to choose a clipping threshold
\tau\in(0,1]
and define

\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{Pconf},\tau}(f):=\frac{\pi_{+}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c(X_{i})w(f(X_{i}))\left(1-\frac{f(X_{i})}{\tau\vee r_{i}}\right).

This introduces clipping-induced approximation error but controls the effective envelope of the corrected residuals. The same clipping can be used inside Algorithm
1
by replacing
\rho^{\mathrm{Pconf}}(O;f,w)
with its clipped counterpart.

Appendix D
Proofs for Section
4

D.1
Formal PU guarantee for WLMC

Here we prove a more general result than the informal main-text statement.

Theorem 3

(Formal PU guarantee under Rademacher complexity)
.

Assume the PU setting of Proposition
1
. Suppose

|c|\leq 1
,
\|w\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, and
\mathcal{W}
is finite. Assume further that the Rademacher complexities are bounded as

\mathfrak{R}_{n_{+}}(\mathcal{C};P_{+})\leq C_{R}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\mathcal{C}}\log n_{+}}{n_{+}}},\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{n_{u}}(\mathcal{C};P_{X})\leq C_{R}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\mathcal{C}}\log n_{u}}{n_{u}}}.

Let

D_{0}=\mathbb{E}[(f_{0}(X)-f^{\star}(X))^{2}]
and

K_{\varepsilon}=\lceil 4D_{0}/\varepsilon^{2}\rceil
. Run Algorithm
1
with

\lambda=\varepsilon/2
, threshold
3\varepsilon/4
, and the PU estimator

\widehat{R}^{\mathrm{PU}}
. At each search call, draw fresh independent batches

S_{t}^{+}\sim P_{+}^{n_{+}}
and
S_{t}^{u}\sim P_{X}^{n_{u}}
. Let

L=\log\frac{4|\mathcal{W}|(K_{\varepsilon}+1)}{\delta}.

There exists
C>0
, depending only on
C_{R}
, such that if

n_{+}\geq\frac{C\pi_{+}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}(d_{\mathcal{C}}\log n_{+}+L),\qquad n_{u}\geq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}(d_{\mathcal{C}}\log n_{u}+L),

then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the algorithm stops
automatically after

T\leq K_{\varepsilon}=O(D_{0}/\varepsilon^{2})
successful updates and returns a

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W},\varepsilon)
-multicalibrated predictor
f_{T}
.
In particular, if
n_{+}=n_{u}=n
, it suffices that

n\geq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left[d_{\mathcal{C}}\log n+\log|\mathcal{W}|+\log\frac{K_{\varepsilon}+1}{\delta}\right].

The total source-wise budgets are

N_{+}\leq(K_{\varepsilon}+1)n_{+}
and

N_{u}\leq(K_{\varepsilon}+1)n_{u}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to
17
. However, we need to control the finite-sample estimation error.

For a fixed predictor
f
, define the population residual moment

R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{WSL}}(f)=\mathbb{E}[c(X)w(f(X))(Y-f(X))]=\mathbb{E}[c(X)w(f(X))(f^{\star}(X)-f(X))].

In the PU setting, the same moment is represented as

R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f)=\pi_{+}\mathbb{E}_{P_{+}}[c(X)w(f(X))]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{X}}[c(X)w(f(X))f(X)].

Consider a population search rule which either returns

(\tau,c,w)\in\{\pm 1\}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{W}
with

\tau R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{WSL}}(f)\geq\varepsilon/2
, or returns
\perp
, in which
case
\sup_{c,w}|R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{WSL}}(f)|\leq\varepsilon
.

Let

\Psi(f)=\mathbb{E}[(f(X)-f^{\star}(X))^{2}].

If the population rule returns
(\tau_{t},c_{t},w_{t})
, the update is

f_{t+1}(x)=\Pi_{[0,1]}\left[f_{t}(x)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\tau_{t}c_{t}(x)w_{t}(f_{t}(x))\right].

Set

\Delta_{t}(x):=\tau_{t}c_{t}(x)w_{t}(f_{t}(x)).

Since
f^{\star}(x)\in[0,1]
and projection onto
[0,1]
is non-expansive,

(f_{t+1}(x)-f^{\star}(x))^{2}\leq\left(f_{t}(x)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Delta_{t}(x)-f^{\star}(x)\right)^{2}.

Taking expectations and expanding the square, we obtain

\displaystyle\Psi(f_{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f_{t}(X)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Delta_{t}(X)-f^{\star}(X)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\Psi(f_{t})+\varepsilon\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{t}(X)(f_{t}(X)-f^{\star}(X))]+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{t}(X)^{2}]

\displaystyle=\Psi(f_{t})-\varepsilon\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{t}(X)(f^{\star}(X)-f_{t}(X))]+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{t}(X)^{2}]

\displaystyle=\Psi(f_{t})-\varepsilon\tau_{t}R_{c_{t},w_{t}}^{\mathrm{WSL}}(f_{t})+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{t}(X)^{2}].

Since
|c_{t}|\leq 1
,
\|w_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, and
\tau_{t}\in\{\pm 1\}
, we have

|\Delta_{t}|\leq 1
. Moreover, the population witness satisfies

\tau_{t}R_{c_{t},w_{t}}^{\mathrm{WSL}}(f_{t})\geq\varepsilon/2.

Therefore,

\Psi(f_{t+1})\leq\Psi(f_{t})-\varepsilon\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}=\Psi(f_{t})-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}.

Thus every successful population update decreases the potential by at least

\varepsilon^{2}/4
. After
M
successful updates,

\Psi(f_{M})\leq D_{0}-\frac{M\varepsilon^{2}}{4}.

Since
\Psi(f)\geq 0
for every predictor
f
, we must have

M\leq\frac{4D_{0}}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Otherwise the potential would become negative, which is impossible. Hence the
population procedure cannot keep finding valid update directions forever; after
at most
4D_{0}/\varepsilon^{2}
successful updates, it must fail to find another
valid witness and therefore stops. In particular, the number of successful
population updates is at most
K_{\varepsilon}=\lceil 4D_{0}/\varepsilon^{2}\rceil
.

Next, we control the finite-sample estimation error. Fix a round
t
and condition on the past. Since the batches at round
t

are fresh,
f_{t}
is fixed independently of
S_{t}^{+}
and
S_{t}^{u}
. Define

\mathcal{E}_{t}=\left\{\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},w\in\mathcal{W}}\left|\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f_{t};S_{t}^{+},S_{t}^{u})-R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f_{t})\right|\leq\varepsilon/4\right\}.

On
\mathcal{E}_{t}
, if the empirical search returns a witness with

\tau_{t}\widehat{R}_{c_{t},w_{t}}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f_{t})>3\varepsilon/4,

then

\tau_{t}R_{c_{t},w_{t}}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f_{t})>\varepsilon/2.

Hence the update is a valid population update and decreases
\Psi
by at
least
\varepsilon^{2}/4
. Conversely, if the empirical search stops, then

\sup_{c,w}|\widehat{R}_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f_{t})|\leq 3\varepsilon/4,

and therefore
\sup_{c,w}|R_{c,w}^{\mathrm{PU}}(f_{t})|\leq\varepsilon
. Thus the
output is
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W},\varepsilon)
-multicalibrated.

Apply the finite-
\mathcal{W}
form of Proposition
1
with
failure probability
\delta/(K_{\varepsilon}+1)
. Using the assumed
Rademacher bound, there exists
C>0
, depending only on
C_{R}
, such that

\Pr(\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c})\leq\delta/(K_{\varepsilon}+1)
whenever

n_{+}\geq\frac{C\pi_{+}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}(d_{\mathcal{C}}\log n_{+}+L),\qquad n_{u}\geq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}(d_{\mathcal{C}}\log n_{u}+L),

where
L=\log(4|\mathcal{W}|(K_{\varepsilon}+1)/\delta)
.

By a union bound over the first
K_{\varepsilon}+1
search calls,

\Pr\left(\bigcap_{t=0}^{K_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{E}_{t}\right)\geq 1-\delta.

On this event, every empirical update before stopping is a valid population
update. Since more than
K_{\varepsilon}
successful updates are impossible,
the no-horizon algorithm stops automatically within the first

K_{\varepsilon}+1
search calls. The sample budgets follow by multiplying
the per-call source-wise sample sizes by the number of search calls:

N_{+}\leq(K_{\varepsilon}+1)n_{+},\qquad N_{u}\leq(K_{\varepsilon}+1)n_{u}.

This proves the theorem.
∎

Finite
\mathcal{C}
case.

When
\mathcal{C}
is finite, the same proof applies. Only Step 4 changes: instead
of the Rademacher bound, we use Hoeffding’s inequality and a union bound over

\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{W}
. Consequently, the extra
d_{\mathcal{C}}\log n
term is
replaced by
\log|\mathcal{C}|
; in particular, no additional
\log n
factor is
needed.

Theorem 4

(Formal PU guarantee for finite classes)
.

Assume the PU setting of Proposition
1
. Suppose

\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{W}
are finite, and
|c|\leq 1
,
\|w\|_{\infty}\leq 1
.
Let
D_{0}=\mathbb{E}[(f_{0}(X)-f^{\star}(X))^{2}]
and

K_{\varepsilon}=\lceil 4D_{0}/\varepsilon^{2}\rceil
. Run
Algorithm
1
with
\lambda=\varepsilon/2
, threshold

3\varepsilon/4
, and the PU estimator
\widehat{R}^{\mathrm{PU}}
. At each search
call, draw fresh independent batches
S_{t}^{+}\sim P_{+}^{n_{+}}
and

S_{t}^{u}\sim P_{X}^{n_{u}}
. Let

L_{\rm fin}=\log\frac{4|\mathcal{C}||\mathcal{W}|(K_{\varepsilon}+1)}{\delta}.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that if

n_{+}\geq\frac{C\pi_{+}^{2}L_{\rm fin}}{\varepsilon^{2}},\qquad n_{u}\geq\frac{CL_{\rm fin}}{\varepsilon^{2}},

then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the algorithm stops
automatically after

T\leq K_{\varepsilon}=O(D_{0}/\varepsilon^{2})
successful updates and returns a

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W},\varepsilon)
-multicalibrated predictor
f_{T}
.

In particular, if
n_{+}=n_{u}=n
, it suffices that

n\geq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log\frac{4|\mathcal{C}||\mathcal{W}|(K_{\varepsilon}+1)}{\delta}.

The total source-wise budgets are

N_{+}\leq(K_{\varepsilon}+1)n_{+}
and

N_{u}\leq(K_{\varepsilon}+1)n_{u}
; in the equal-size case,

N_{+}=N_{u}=\widetilde{O}\!\left(\left(1+\frac{D_{0}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\frac{\log|\mathcal{C}|+\log|\mathcal{W}|+\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

D.2
Other weak-supervision models

The same argument as in the PU case yields the promised

O(\varepsilon^{-4})
total weak-sample order for the other weak-supervision
examples. We record the argument as a proof sketch, since no new boosting
idea is needed beyond replacing the PU uniform-convergence step by the
corresponding one for each weak-observation model.

Suppose a weak-supervision model has source indices
j\in J
, source laws

Q_{j}
, per-source batch sizes
m_{j}
, and envelope constants
B_{j}
.
Assume that, for every predictor
f
fixed independently of the fresh weak
data, the corrected residual process satisfies the uniform-convergence bound

\displaystyle\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C},\,w\in\mathcal{W}}\left|\widehat{R}^{\mathrm{WSL}}_{c,w}(f)-R^{\mathrm{WSL}}_{c,w}(f)\right|

(62)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{j\in J}B_{j}\left(\mathfrak{R}_{m_{j}}(\mathcal{C};Q_{j})+\eta_{w}+\sqrt{\frac{2\log N_{\infty}(\eta_{w},\mathcal{W})+\log(1/\delta_{0})}{m_{j}}}\right).

(63)

This process bound implies the analogous bound for

\bigl|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{\mathrm{WSL}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)-\operatorname{MC}^{\mathrm{WSL}}_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W}}(f)\bigr|
.
If
\mathfrak{R}_{m}(\mathcal{C};Q_{j})\leq\sqrt{d_{\mathcal{C}}/m}
for all
j
, then choosing

m_{j}\geq C\,\frac{B_{j}^{2}\bigl(d_{\mathcal{C}}+2\log N_{\infty}(\varepsilon/8,\mathcal{W})+\log(K_{\varepsilon}/\delta)\bigr)}{\varepsilon^{2}},\qquad K_{\varepsilon}=\lceil 4/\varepsilon^{2}\rceil,

ensures that every round has a correct weak agnostic learner with probability at least
1-\delta
. Consequently WLMC terminates after
O(\varepsilon^{-2})
successful updates and uses

N_{j}=O\!\left(\frac{B_{j}^{2}\bigl(d_{\mathcal{C}}+2\log N_{\infty}(\varepsilon/8,\mathcal{W})+\log(K_{\varepsilon}/\delta)\bigr)}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right)

fresh weak samples from source
Q_{j}
. For UU,
J=\{1,2\}
,
Q_{j}=P_{U_{j}}
, and
B_{j}=A_{j}
, where the
constants
A_{j}
are the source-wise envelopes appearing in the UU/MCD
uniform-convergence bound. For Pconf,
J=\{+\}
,
Q_{+}=P_{+}
, and under the
lower-confidence condition
r(x)\geq C_{r}
, one may take

B_{+}=\pi_{+}(1+1/C_{r}).

Proof sketch.

The proof has the same two steps as the PU proof.

First, consider the population version of WLMC. By the weak-supervision
rewrite for the model under consideration, the corrected weak moment

R^{\mathrm{WSL}}_{c,w}(f)
is equal to the clean multicalibration residual

\mathbb{E}\!\left[c(X)w(f(X))(Y-f(X))\right]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[c(X)w(f(X))(f^{\star}(X)-f(X))\right].

Therefore the deterministic boosting argument is unchanged. Whenever the
population search finds a signed witness
(\tau,c,w)
with

\tau R^{\mathrm{WSL}}_{c,w}(f_{t})\geq\varepsilon/2,

the update with step size
\lambda=\varepsilon/2
decreases the potential

\Psi(f)=\mathbb{E}\!\left[(f(X)-f^{\star}(X))^{2}\right]

by at least
\varepsilon^{2}/4
. Since
0\leq f,f^{\star}\leq 1
, we have

\Psi(f_{0})\leq 1
, and hence there can be at most

K_{\varepsilon}=\lceil 4/\varepsilon^{2}\rceil

successful population updates. If no such population witness exists, then the
current predictor is
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W},\varepsilon)
-multicalibrated.

Second, we pass from the population search to the empirical weak search. At
each round WLMC uses fresh weak data, so conditional on the past, the current
predictor
f_{t}
is fixed independently of the batch used at round
t
.
Thus we may apply the model-specific uniform-convergence bound above with
failure probability

\delta_{0}=\delta/(K_{\varepsilon}+1).

Choose
\eta_{w}=\varepsilon/8
. If

\mathfrak{R}_{m}(\mathcal{C};Q_{j})\leq\sqrt{d_{\mathcal{C}}/m}
for all
j
, then choosing

m_{j}\geq C\,\frac{B_{j}^{2}\bigl(d_{\mathcal{C}}+2\log N_{\infty}(\varepsilon/8,\mathcal{W})+\log(K_{\varepsilon}/\delta)\bigr)}{\varepsilon^{2}}

ensures that, in each round,

\sup_{c,w}\left|\widehat{R}^{\mathrm{WSL}}_{c,w}(f_{t})-R^{\mathrm{WSL}}_{c,w}(f_{t})\right|\leq\varepsilon/4

with probability at least
1-\delta/(K_{\varepsilon}+1)
. A union bound over
the first
K_{\varepsilon}+1
search calls gives this event simultaneously for
all rounds with probability at least
1-\delta
.

On this event, the empirical threshold
3\varepsilon/4
has the same role as
in the PU proof. If the empirical search returns a signed witness satisfying

\tau_{t}\widehat{R}^{\mathrm{WSL}}_{c_{t},w_{t}}(f_{t})>3\varepsilon/4,

then

\tau_{t}R^{\mathrm{WSL}}_{c_{t},w_{t}}(f_{t})>\varepsilon/2,

so the empirical update is a valid population update and decreases

\Psi
by at least
\varepsilon^{2}/4
. Conversely, if the empirical search
stops, then

\sup_{c,w}\left|R^{\mathrm{WSL}}_{c,w}(f_{t})\right|\leq\varepsilon,

and the output is
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W},\varepsilon)
-multicalibrated. Since there
are at most
K_{\varepsilon}=O(\varepsilon^{-2})
successful updates, the total
number of fresh weak samples drawn from source
Q_{j}
is

N_{j}=O\!\left(\frac{B_{j}^{2}\bigl(d_{\mathcal{C}}+2\log N_{\infty}(\varepsilon/8,\mathcal{W})+\log(K_{\varepsilon}/\delta)\bigr)}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right).

∎

Thus the same two-step proof applies to each weak setting: the population
boosting decrease is identical after the weak residual rewrite, and the only
model-specific ingredient is the corresponding uniform-convergence bound for
the corrected weak residuals.

D.3
Corrected weak objectives for Platt and temperature scaling

Let
g_{\theta}
denote either
g_{\beta}
or
g_{a,b}
, and write

\ell_{\theta}^{+}(x):=-\log g_{\theta}(x),\qquad\ell_{\theta}^{-}(x):=-\log(1-g_{\theta}(x)).

The fully supervised calibration objective is
\mathbb{E}[\ell_{\theta}^{+}(X)\mathbf{1}\{Y=1\}+\ell_{\theta}^{-}(X)\mathbf{1}\{Y=0\}]
. The weak baselines in Section
4.2
minimize corrected or stabilized empirical versions of this objective. For PU, the unbiased corrected objective is

\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{PU}}(\theta)=\frac{\pi_{+}}{n_{+}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{+}}\{\ell_{\theta}^{+}(X_{i}^{+})-\ell_{\theta}^{-}(X_{i}^{+})\}+\frac{1}{n_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{u}}\ell_{\theta}^{-}(X_{j}^{u}).

In the PU Platt and temperature experiments, we follow the non-negative PU stabilization of
28
and optimize

\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{nnPU}}(\theta)=\frac{\pi_{+}}{n_{+}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{+}}\ell_{\theta}^{+}(X_{i}^{+})+\max\left\{0,\frac{1}{n_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{u}}\ell_{\theta}^{-}(X_{j}^{u})-\frac{\pi_{+}}{n_{+}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{+}}\ell_{\theta}^{-}(X_{i}^{+})\right\}.

This prevents the empirical negative-risk component from becoming negative and mitigates finite-sample overfitting. The stabilization is used only to fit the parametric PU scaling map; PU MC estimation and WLMC auditing use the corrected residual moments. For UU/MCD with
P_{U_{k}}=\theta_{k}P_{+}+(1-\theta_{k})P_{-}
and
\Delta=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}
, the objective is obtained by substituting the log-loss vector into
M_{\mathrm{UU}}^{-1}
:

\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{UU}}(\theta)=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\frac{(1-\theta_{2})\pi_{+}\ell_{\theta}^{+}(X_{i}^{U_{1}})-\theta_{2}\pi_{-}\ell_{\theta}^{-}(X_{i}^{U_{1}})}{\Delta}+\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}\frac{-(1-\theta_{1})\pi_{+}\ell_{\theta}^{+}(X_{j}^{U_{2}})+\theta_{1}\pi_{-}\ell_{\theta}^{-}(X_{j}^{U_{2}})}{\Delta}.

For Pconf, Bayes’ rule gives the corrected positive-sample objective

\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{Pconf}}(\theta)=\frac{\pi_{+}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\ell_{\theta}^{+}(X_{i})+\frac{1-r_{i}}{r_{i}}\ell_{\theta}^{-}(X_{i})\right\}.

In experiments, these objectives are optimized over the one-dimensional temperature parameter or the two-dimensional Platt parameters on a held-out weak calibration split, keeping the base predictor fixed. The correctness proof is the same WSL decontamination argument used above: fix the loss vector, rewrite its population risk through the appropriate weak-observation inverse or confidence weight, and then replace the resulting observable expectations by their empirical averages.

Appendix E
Settings of Toy Data Experiments

The toy experiment in Figure
1
uses a one-dimensional binary classification problem with a known data-generating distribution. We draw
X\sim\mathrm{Unif}([0,1])
and evaluate a fixed probabilistic predictor

f(x)=\operatorname{clip}(0.12+0.76x,10^{-6},1-10^{-6}).

The true conditional label probability is

r(x)=\Pr(Y=1\mid X=x)=\operatorname{clip}\{f(x)+0.11\sin(2\pi x),0.02,0.98\},

and labels follow
Y\mid X=x\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(r(x))
. The prevalence is
\pi_{+}=\mathbb{E}[r(X)]
. The evaluated finite class is formed by crossing eight equal-width subgroups
\mathcal{C}=\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{8}\}
partitioning
\mathcal{X}
(
[0,1]
) with ten equal-width score bins
\mathcal{W}=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{10}\}
on
[0,1]
. Thus each witness cell is

a_{g,b}(x;f):=\mathbf{1}\{x\in G_{g}\}\mathbf{1}\{f(x)\in B_{b}\}.

For this diagnostic, the population target is the finite-class multicalibration moment

\operatorname{MC}_{\mathrm{toy}}(f):=\max_{g\in[8],\,b\in[10]}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[a_{g,b}(X;f)(Y-f(X))\right]\right|=\max_{g\in[8],\,b\in[10]}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[a_{g,b}(X;f)(r(X)-f(X))\right]\right|.

This is the unnormalized residual moment used in the multicalibration definition; it is not divided by the mass of the corresponding cell. The population value is computed numerically using a grid of 400,000 points over
[0,1]
. The plotted sample sizes are
128,256,512,1024,2048,4096,8192,16384,32768
, and
65536
.

For each sample size, independent Monte Carlo repetitions are used to construct PN, Pconf, PU, and UU estimates of the same target. The PN estimate uses labeled marginal samples
(X_{i},Y_{i})
and the empirical cell moments
n^{-1}\sum_{i}a_{g,b}(X_{i};f)(Y_{i}-f(X_{i}))
. The Pconf estimate uses positive samples
X_{i}^{+}\sim P(X\mid Y=1)
with the exact confidence
r(X_{i}^{+})
and the corrected moments

\frac{\pi_{+}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{g,b}(X_{i}^{+};f)\left(1-\frac{f(X_{i}^{+})}{r(X_{i}^{+})}\right).

The PU estimate uses positive samples and marginal unlabeled samples
X_{j}^{u}\sim P_{X}
:

\frac{\pi_{+}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{g,b}(X_{i}^{+};f)-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{g,b}(X_{j}^{u};f)f(X_{j}^{u}).

The UU estimate uses two unlabeled mixtures
P_{U_{k}}=\rho_{k}P_{+}+(1-\rho_{k})P_{-}
with
\rho_{1}=0.80
and
\rho_{2}=0.20
, where
P_{+}=P(X\mid Y=1)
and
P_{-}=P(X\mid Y=0)
, and applies the corresponding two-source decontamination weights to estimate the same cell moments.

For a method
q\in\{\mathrm{PN},\mathrm{Pconf},\mathrm{PU},\mathrm{UU}\}
, sample size
n
, and repetition
s
, let
\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{q,(s)}_{n}
be the corresponding estimate. The plotted point is the mean absolute estimation error

\frac{1}{R}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\left|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{q,(s)}_{n}-\operatorname{MC}_{\mathrm{toy}}(f)\right|,

where
R
is the number of Monte Carlo repetitions at that sample size; in the plotted experiment,
R=10
. The vertical error bar is one standard deviation across those absolute errors,

\left[\frac{1}{R}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\left(\left|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{q,(s)}_{n}-\operatorname{MC}_{\mathrm{toy}}(f)\right|-\frac{1}{R}\sum_{t=1}^{R}\left|\widehat{\operatorname{MC}}^{q,(t)}_{n}-\operatorname{MC}_{\mathrm{toy}}(f)\right|\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}.

Thus the error bars show variability across Monte Carlo repetitions; they are not confidence intervals or standard errors. The gray guide lines in the plot have slope
-1/2
on the log–log scale and indicate the parametric finite-sample rate predicted by the concentration bounds.

Appendix F
Dataset and Subgroup Descriptions

This appendix describes the real-data evaluation setting used in Section
6
. We follow the finite-subgroup viewpoint common in practical multicalibration experiments: rather than searching over an abstract infinite class, we evaluate a fixed collection of interpretable groups defined by dataset metadata or tabular attributes. The groups may overlap, so a point can belong to several evaluated subpopulations.

We first define the evaluation metrics, subgroup collections, and data splits. Appendix
G
then gives the authoritative split-first construction of the weak observation views used by those metrics. Later appendices summarize the model and post-processing hyperparameters, reproducibility details, and detailed result tables.

F.1
Calibration and multicalibration metrics

MC is the unnormalized witness moment appearing in the theory, so small subgroup–bin cells can have small absolute moments even when their conditional calibration error is large. To compare subpopulations fairly, we also report MaxECE: it normalizes each subgroup’s binned calibration error by the subgroup mass and then takes the worst subgroup. Following
20
, our numerical experiments report ECE and MaxECE in addition to MC. Let
f:\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
be a frozen predictor, let
B_{1},\ldots,B_{K}
be score bins, and let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{m}
be the evaluated subgroup collection. We also write
G_{0}=\mathcal{X}
for the whole population and

a_{g,b}(x;f):=\mathbf{1}\{x\in G_{g}\}\mathbf{1}\{f(x)\in B_{b}\}.

The superscript
\mathrm{or}
denotes oracle clean-label quantities; in the fully supervised PN setting, these coincide with the corresponding full-label estimates.
For a group–bin cell, define the following moment and group mass

m^{\rm or}_{g,b}(f):=\mathbb{E}\!\big[a_{g,b}(X;f)(Y-f(X))\big],\qquad\mu_{g}:=\Pr(X\in G_{g}).

(64)

Given a clean sample
S=\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, the corresponding oracle empirical quantities are

\widehat{m}^{\rm or}_{g,b}(f):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{g,b}(X_{i};f)(Y_{i}-f(X_{i})),\qquad\widehat{\mu}^{\rm or}_{g}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}\in G_{g}\}.

(65)

The oracle ECE, maxECE, and MC columns in the tables are computed as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{ECE}}^{\rm or}(f)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{b=1}^{K}\left|\widehat{m}^{\rm or}_{0,b}(f)\right|,

(66)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{maxECE}}^{\rm or}(f)

\displaystyle:=\max_{g\in[m]:\,\widehat{\mu}^{\rm or}_{g}>\mu_{\min}}\frac{1}{\widehat{\mu}^{\rm or}_{g}}\sum_{b=1}^{K}\left|\widehat{m}^{\rm or}_{g,b}(f)\right|,

(67)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{MC}}^{\rm or}(f)

\displaystyle:=\max_{g\in[m],\,b\in[K]}\left|\widehat{m}^{\rm or}_{g,b}(f)\right|.

(68)

Here
\mu_{\min}
is the minimum active-group mass used by the implementation; setting
\mu_{\min}=0
includes all groups with positive empirical mass. The ECE formula is the usual binned ECE because
\left|\widehat{m}^{\rm or}_{0,b}\right|
equals the bin mass times the absolute difference between the average label and average prediction in bin
B_{b}
. The maxECE formula applies the same binned ECE calculation conditionally within each subgroup and then takes the worst subgroup. MC instead keeps the supremum-style unnormalized group–bin residual used by the witness definition.

Weak estimates of ECE, maxECE, and MC.

Appendix
G
specifies how the held-out validation and test splits are converted into Pconf, PU, and UU views. Here we only define the corrected group–bin residuals that are plugged into the metrics and into the WLMC audit. For weak-observation model
q\in\{\mathrm{PU},\mathrm{UU},\mathrm{Pconf}\}
, define
\widehat{m}^{q}_{g,b}(f)
by applying the corrected witness estimator to
c(x)=\mathbf{1}\{x\in G_{g}\}
and
w(v)=\mathbf{1}\{v\in B_{b}\}
. Thus, for PU data
(S_{+},S_{u})
,

\widehat{m}^{\mathrm{PU}}_{g,b}(f)=\frac{\pi_{+}}{n_{+}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{+}}a_{g,b}(X_{i}^{+};f)-\frac{1}{n_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{u}}a_{g,b}(X_{j}^{u};f)f(X_{j}^{u}).

(69)

For UU data with
U_{k}=\rho_{k}P_{+}+(1-\rho_{k})P_{-}
and
\Delta=\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\neq 0
,

\widehat{m}^{\mathrm{UU}}_{g,b}(f)=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\alpha_{k}(f(X_{i}^{U_{k}}))a_{g,b}(X_{i}^{U_{k}};f),

(70)

where

\alpha_{1}(v)=\frac{(1-\rho_{2})\pi_{+}(1-v)+\rho_{2}\pi_{-}v}{\Delta},\qquad\alpha_{2}(v)=\frac{-(1-\rho_{1})\pi_{+}(1-v)-\rho_{1}\pi_{-}v}{\Delta}.

For Pconf data
\{(X_{i},r_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
with
X_{i}\sim P_{+}
and
r_{i}\approx\Pr(Y=1\mid X_{i})
,

\widehat{m}^{\mathrm{Pconf}}_{g,b}(f)=\frac{\pi_{+}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{g,b}(X_{i};f)\left(1-\frac{f(X_{i})}{r_{i}}\right),

(71)

with
r_{i}
replaced by
\max\{r_{i},\tau\}
if confidence clipping is enabled.

The WLMC audit uses exactly this corrected residual table: it evaluates the finite subgroup–bin family, finds the largest corrected signed residual, and applies the corresponding post-processing update. Appendix
G
supplements this definition by specifying which split-specific source indices, confidence values, and subgroup masks enter the Pconf, PU, and UU versions of the table.

The weak ECE, maxECE, and MC columns use the same aggregation rules as Eq. (
66
), replacing oracle cell moments by the corrected weak moments above. For group-wise maxECE, the denominator is the target covariate group mass computed from the full feature-only held-out evaluation split, denoted
\widehat{\mu}_{g}^{\rm eval}
and defined in Eq. (
75
). Hence, for
q\in\{\mathrm{PU},\mathrm{UU},\mathrm{Pconf}\}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{ECE}}^{q}(f)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{b=1}^{K}\left|\widehat{m}^{q}_{0,b}(f)\right|,

(72)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{maxECE}}^{q}(f)

\displaystyle:=\max_{g\in[m]:\,\widehat{\mu}_{g}^{\rm eval}>\mu_{\min}}\frac{1}{\widehat{\mu}_{g}^{\rm eval}}\sum_{b=1}^{K}\left|\widehat{m}^{q}_{g,b}(f)\right|,

(73)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{MC}}^{q}(f)

\displaystyle:=\max_{g\in[m],\,b\in[K]}\left|\widehat{m}^{q}_{g,b}(f)\right|.

(74)

This section therefore fixes the metric definitions; Appendix
G
is the source of truth for the split-first weak-data construction, the unbiasedness of
\widehat{\mu}_{g}^{\rm eval}
in our semi-synthetic experiments, and the alternative Pconf/PU/UU denominators needed when no full target covariate evaluation pool is available. Oracle estimates are used only for reporting and analysis, while weak estimates are used for weak validation and weak post-processing selection.

F.2

Table

summarizes the real-data benchmark setting. For the finite subgroup family, we adopt the dataset-specific subgroup collections used by the clean-label multicalibration benchmark of
20
; the subgroup family is not tuned separately for our weak-supervision experiments. Each loader defines a finite, interpretable collection of protected-attribute groups, identity-mention groups, facial-attribute groups, and simple intersections. The same code-level group indicators are used for oracle metrics, weak estimates, validation-based method selection, WLMC audits, and the CDF/scatter diagnostics in Appendix
K.4
. All MC evaluations use 10 score bins, so the number of evaluated group–bin cells is
10m
, where
m
is the number of groups reported in Table
1
. Table
1
gives short descriptions of the loader-defined subgroup collections; the exact code-level definitions are provided in the reproducibility code.

F.3
Data splits and effective sample sizes

The test split is created first with seed
42
. The validation split is then created from the remaining data using the run seed. Post-processing uses a separate correction split carved out of the training partition with seed
50
. The tabular data uses correction fraction
0.4
within the training partition, giving roughly
0.36n
base-training examples and
0.24n
correction examples. CelebA and CivilComments use correction fraction
0.2
, giving roughly
0.48n
base-training examples and
0.12n
correction examples. In all cases, validation and test use roughly
0.20n
each. These split sizes refer to the underlying fully labeled data before labels are hidden; Appendix
G
explains how PN, Pconf, PU, and UU views are generated separately within each split.
